Cosmological Dressing Rules
Chandramouli Chowdhury, Arthur Lipstein, Joe Marshall, Jiajie Mei, and Ivo Sachs

Abstract
The basic observables in cosmology are known as in-in correlators. Recent calculations have revealed that in-in correlators in four dimensional de Sitter space exhibit hidden simplicity stemming from a close relation to scattering amplitudes in flat space. In this paper we explain how to make this property manifest by dressing flat space Feynman diagrams with certain auxiliary propagators. These dressing rules are derived for conformally coupled and massless scalar theories and we show that they reproduce the same infrared divergences predicted by the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism.



LMU-ASC 03/25

1
Introduction

The calculation of correlation functions in curved space time is complicated by the absence of Poincare invariance on which many of the powerful tools of computing flat space amplitudes rely. On the other hand, one should be able to reach a similar level of sophistication for highly symmetric curved spacetimes such as de Sitter (dS) space since it has the same number of isometries as Minkowski space. This background is of particular interest in cosmology because it approximately describes the early Universe during inflation
Guth (1981)
;
Linde (1982)
;
Albrecht and Steinhardt (1982)
. Yet the standard tools of momentum space still have to be modified due to the absence of time-translation invariance, implying, in particular, that
in
and
out
vacua are not identical. The canonical approach for in-in calculations with time-dependent backgrounds is the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism
Keldysh (1964)
;
Schwinger (1961)
;
Maldacena (2003)
;
Weinberg (2005)
. Alternatively, one may start with the cosmological wavefunction
Hartle and Hawking (1983)
, which can be perturbatively obtained from analytic continuation of Witten diagrams in Anti de Sitter (AdS) space
Maldacena (2003)
;
McFadden and Skenderis (2010)
;
Maldacena and Pimentel (2011)
, and then compute cosmological correlators as expectation values. In neither of the two approaches are tools of flat space Feynman integrals manifestly applicable. Nevertheless, there has been a great deal of work in recent years to bootstrap wavefunction coefficients using ideas inspired by the study of scattering amplitudes in flat space
Raju (2012b)
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. (2017)
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. (2020)
;
Benincasa (2018)
;
Sleight and Taronna (2020)
;
Baumann et al. (2021)
;
Goodhew et al. (2021)
;
Gomez et al. (2021)
;
Jazayeri et al. (2021)
;
Melville and Pajer (2021)
;
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
;
Bonifacio et al. (2023)
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. (2023)
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. (2024)
;
Melville and Pimentel (2024)
;
Baumann et al. (2025)
;
Glew and Lukowski (2025)
;
Cespedes and Jazayeri (2025)
;
Goodhew et al. (2024)
.
In this paper we will argue that in-in correlators are actually simpler and more closely related to scattering amplitudes than wavefunction coefficients.

There is an equivalent way to calculate cosmological correlators in terms of an effective action in Euclidean AdS, at the cost of introducing a doubled set of extra (
shadow
) fields
Sleight and Taronna (2021a)
;
Sleight and Taronna (2021b)
;
Di Pietro et al. (2022)
. For the conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
theory,
Chowdhury et al. (2023)
showed that individual loop-level diagrams in this formalism have the same complexity as a wavefunction coefficients, but after summing over all contributing diagrams, a flat space structure appears somewhat miraculously, allowing one to use standard methods for flat space integrals for calculation. In subsequent work
Donath and Pajer (2024)
, it was shown that in-in correlators of certain IR-finite scalar theories can be mapped to in-out correlators. For other recent work relating cosmological correlators to scattering amplitudes see
Bonifacio et al. (2021)
;
Benincasa and Dian (2024)
.

One may then wonder if the close relation to scattering amplitudes is a generic feature of cosmological correlators. The aim of this paper is to argue that this is indeed the case and to propose a set of rules that allow one to describe correlators in a large class of scalar field theories by dressing the corresponding flat space diagrams with auxiliary propagators. The dressing rules are formulated at the integrand level and hold to all orders in perturbation theory. Quite remarkably, in all the cases where we are able to carry out the integrals, we find that the analytic structure of the final result can also be read off from the dressing rules in a way that we will make more precise later on.
To illustrate the power of the dressing rules,
we evaluate the tree-level five-point correlator of conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory which has not appeared in the literature before (moreover the methods of
Donath and Pajer (2024)
do not apply to this theory even though it is IR finite). We find that its analytic structure is considerably simpler than that of the wavefunction
Hillman (2019)
and is made manifest by the dressing rules.

The dressing rules are also applicable to massless scalars in de Sitter, which is perhaps of more phenomenological interest. Such correlation functions are well known to feature infrared divergencies
Birrell and Davies (1982)
;
Maldacena (2003)
;
Tsamis and Woodard (1994)
;
Beneke and Moch (2013)
which need to be regularized. In contrast to a hard cut-off in conformal time often used in the literature
1
1

1

We should note that for phenomenological applications, such as a finite duration of the inflationary phase, a non-invariant cut-off may be physically motivated
Senatore and Zaldarriaga (2010)
. It is also worth noting that there is a modification of the hard-cutoff, known as the
comoving cutoff
, which can be used to obtain dS invariant correlators
Baumgart and Sundrum (2020)
.

Anninos et al. (2015)
, we will primarily use dimensional regularization. It turns out that a dimensional regularization is required to derive the dressing rules from the shadow formalism
2
2

2

We would like to the thank the authors of
Di Pietro et al. (2022)
for suggesting to consider non-integer dimensions in the shadow formalism.
and we show that with this regularization the dressing rules apply and are indeed equivalent to Schwinger-Keldysh calculations. Note that a regulator is also required for UV divergences. In general, standard regulators like a cut-off or dimensional regularization will break the de Sitter symmetry but there is an alternative known as analytic regularization which preserves this property, although it arises from a non-local deformation of the action. Nevertheless, dressing rules can also be defined for this regulator, as explained in Appendix
A
.

The ability to express in-in correlators in terms of dressed flat space Feynman diagrams allows one to make use of standard techniques for computing flat space Feynman integrals, as demonstrated in Appendix
D
. While dressing rules can also be defined for wavefunction coefficients, they take a more complicated form, which reflects the underlying additional complexity of wavefunctions, as discussed in appendix
E
.

The paper is organized as follows. In section
2.1
we review the standard formalisms for computing in-in correlators in dS. Next, in section
2.2
we illustrate the main ideas of the paper through a few simple examples. This section also provides a detailed summary of the “dressing rules” developed in the rest of the paper. In section
3
we derive the dressing rules from the shadow effective action for a variety of theories by considering numerous examples. In section
4
these rules are then applied to the conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory to obtain a compact expression for the tree level five-point correlator. In sections
5
and
6
we compare the dressing rules with Schwinger-Keldysh formalism and the wavefunction approach, respectively, and find complete agreement in particular for IR divergent examples. Finally, we present our conclusions in section
7
. We also provide some Appendices with various technical details. In particular we discuss propagators in
A
, provide a general derivation for the dressing rules for power law potentials in
B
, calculate various integrals in
C
and
D
, and derive dressing rules for wavefunction coefficients in
E
.

2
Overview

In this section, we will briefly review cosmological wavefunctions and in-in correlators, and how to compute the latter using the shadow formalism. We then provide an invitation to the dressing rules, illustrating how they arise in a few simple examples.

2.1
Review

We will work in the Poincaré patch of dS
4
equipped with the metric

{\rm d}s^{2}=\ell_{dS}^{2}{-{\rm d}\eta^{2}+{\rm d}\vec{x}^{2}\over\eta^{2}},

(1)

where
\ell_{dS}
denotes the curvature radius (which is henceforth set to
1
),
-\infty<\eta<0
is the conformal time, and
\vec{x}
denotes the Euclidean boundary directions with individual components
x^{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. Cosmological (or in-in) correlators of a scalar field
\phi
are given by

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\frac{\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\phi(\vec{k}_{n})\left|\Psi\left[\phi\right]\right|^{2}}{\int\mathcal{D}\phi\left|\Psi\left[\phi\right]\right|^{2}},

(2)

where
\phi
represents the value of a generic bulk field in the future boundary Fourier transformed to momentum space,
\vec{k}_{a}
with
a\in\left\{1,...,n\right\}
are boundary momenta, and
\Psi\left[\phi\right]
is the cosmological wavefunction
Hartle and Hawking (1983)
, which is a functional of
\phi
. The wavefunction
\Psi\left[\phi\right]
in turn, has the perturbative expansion

\ln\Psi\left[\phi\right]=-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\rm d}^{d}k_{i}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\delta^{d}(\sum_{i}^{n}\vec{k}_{i})\Psi_{n}\left(\vec{k}_{1},\dots,\vec{k}_{n}\right)\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\phi(\vec{k}_{n})\,,

(3)

where
d
is the number of boundary dimensions. In practice, we set
d=3+2\epsilon
in dimensional regularization. The wavefunction coefficients
\Psi_{n}
can be related to CFT correlators in the future boundary
Maldacena (2003)
;
Maldacena and Pimentel (2011)
;
McFadden and Skenderis (2010)
;
Bzowski et al. (2013)
;
Bzowski et al. (2014)
;
Bzowski et al. (2018a)
;
Bzowski et al. (2018b)
;
Bzowski et al. (2021)
.
Note that in de Sitter momentum space, momentum is conserved along the boundary but the total energy, defined as

E=\sum_{a=1}^{n}k_{a}\;;\quad k_{a}=|\vec{k}_{a}|,

(4)

is not conserved. In the following, we will not write out the delta functions which impose momentum conservation along the boundary.

Alternatively,
in-in
correlators can also be computed using the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism
Keldysh (1964)
;
Schwinger (1961)
. We will spell out the detailed Feynman rules for Schwinger-Keldysh computations in section
5
. It was shown that the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism can be mapped via analytic continuation to Witten diagrams in Euclidean anti-de Sitter space (EAdS)
Sleight (2020)
;
Sleight and Taronna (2021a)
;
Sleight and Taronna (2021b)
. For a single scalar field with polynomial interactions in dS, the resulting Feynman rules are conveniently encoded by a
shadow
effective action
Di Pietro et al. (2022)
containing two fields,
\phi_{+}
and
\phi_{-}
, given by

iS=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}z{\rm d}^{d}x}{z^{d+1}}\left[\sin\left(\pi(\frac{d}{2}-\Delta_{+})\right)\left((\partial\phi_{+})^{2}\!-\!m^{2}{\phi_{+}}^{2}\right)\right.\cr+\sin\left(\pi(\frac{d}{2}-\Delta_{-})\right)\left((\partial\phi_{-})^{2}\!-\!m^{2}{\phi_{-}}^{2}\right)\cr\left.+e^{i\pi\frac{d-1}{2}}V\left(e^{-i\frac{\pi}{2}\Delta_{+}}\phi_{+}+e^{-i\frac{\pi}{2}\Delta^{-}}\phi_{-}\right)+e^{-i\pi\frac{d-1}{2}}V\left(e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\Delta_{+}}\phi_{+}+e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\Delta_{-}}\phi_{-}\right)\right],

(5)

where
0<z<\infty
is the radial cooridinate of EAdS,
V
is the scalar potential, and
\Delta_{\pm}
are the scaling dimensions of the dual CFT operators, which are related to the mass of the scalar fields
\phi_{\pm}
via

\displaystyle\Delta_{\pm}=\frac{d}{2}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}-m^{2}}\,.

(6)

Note that the kinetic terms of
\phi_{\pm}
have opposite sign. The propagators of ghost-like fields have an additional minus sign, as we will spell out below.

(a)

\phi^{-}
bulk-boundary propagator.

(b)

\phi^{+}
bulk-bulk propagator.

(c)

\phi^{-}
bulk-bulk propagator.

Figure 1
:
Diagrammatic representation of propagators which feature in the shadow formalism, the formulae for which are given in (
7
).
\phi_{+}
fields have
\nu>0
and
\phi_{-}
fields have
\nu<0
. Only the latter appear as external legs.

The effective action above describes how cosmological correlation functions in dS are expressed in terms of EAdS fields. The propagators arising from the EAdS action are given for conformally coupled and massless fields as
Liu and Tseytlin (1999)
;
Raju (2011)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\nu}(z,\vec{k})&=-\sin\left(\pi\nu\right)z^{d/2}\frac{2^{-\nu}k^{\nu}}{\Gamma(1+\nu)}K_{\nu}(kz)~,\\
G_{\nu}(z,z^{\prime};\vec{k})&=-\sin\left(\pi\nu\right)\int dp\,\frac{p}{p^{2}+\vec{k}^{2}}(z\,z^{\prime})^{d/2}J_{\nu}(pz)J_{\nu}(pz^{\prime})\\
&=-\sin(\pi\nu)(zz^{\prime})^{d/2}\Bigg\{\Theta(z-z^{\prime})K_{\nu}(kz)I_{\nu}(kz^{\prime})+\Theta(z^{\prime}-z)I_{\nu}(kz)K_{\nu}(kz^{\prime})\Bigg\}~,\end{split}

(7)

where
K_{\nu}
and
J_{\nu}
are Bessel functions. The overall sign implements whether the field is ghost-like or not. The propagators are depicted in Figure
1
. In particular
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}(z,\vec{k})
denotes a bulk-boundary propagator and
G_{\nu}(z,z^{\prime};\vec{k})
denotes the bulk-bulk propagator with
\nu=\Delta-\frac{d}{2}
. When performing dimensional regularization, we shift
d=3\rightarrow d=3+2\epsilon
and
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta+\epsilon
such that
\nu
is preserved. For conformally coupled scalars
\nu=\pm 1/2
, and for massless scalars
\nu=\pm 3/2
. For
\nu=-3/2
, the integrand in (
7
) has a pole at
p=0
, but we define the contour of the
p
integral to exclude this pole (see appendix
A
for more details).

In practice, we only consider diagrams with
\phi_{-}
fields in the external legs, because these diagrams give the dominant contribution as the endpoints of the diagrams approach the future boundary, although subleading contributions are also of interest for studying the dS/CFT correspondence
Heckelbacher et al. (2022)
.

In the following, we will neglect a prefactor of
\eta_{0}^{\Delta_{-}}k_{a}^{2\Delta_{-}-d}
for each external leg (where
\eta_{0}
denotes the end of inflation). It is understood that all of the formulae that follow should be dressed with this prefactor. The factor of
\eta_{0}^{\Delta_{-}}
is not to be confused with a cutoff in the radial integrals which is typically introduced to regularize infrared divergences. Instead, as discuss below, we use dimensional regularisation to regularize such divergences. Throughout this paper we will use the following conventions:

\displaystyle k_{i}\quad\quad\quad

Energy of
i
th
external leg.

\displaystyle p_{i}\quad\quad\quad

Energy of
i
th
internal non-auxiliary propagator.

\displaystyle k_{\rm ext}\quad\quad\quad

Sum of
k_{i}
’s at a vertex.

\displaystyle p_{\rm tot}\quad\quad\quad

Sum of
p_{i}
’s at a vertex.

\displaystyle k_{ij\cdots}\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle|\vec{k}_{i}|+|\vec{k}_{j}|+\cdots

\displaystyle\vec{y}_{ij\cdots}\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle\vec{k}_{i}+\vec{k}_{j}+\cdots

2.2
Invitation to dressing rules

In this paper, we will present a simple set of rules for obtaining in-in correlators of conformally coupled and massless scalars by dressing flat space Feynman diagrams with auxiliary propagators. We will then argue that this provides a systematic way to determine the analytic structure of these correlators. In this section we would like to first motivate the existence of such rules by looking at two simple examples.

It was shown in
Chowdhury et al. (2023)
that the
s
-channel contribution to the 1-loop 4-point in-in correlator in conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
theory is given by

\mathcal{A}_{4}=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\frac{k_{12}k_{34}}{(k_{12}^{2}+p^{2})(k_{34}^{2}+p^{2})}\int d^{4}L\frac{1}{L^{2}(L+K)^{2}}\,,

(9)

where
K^{\mu}=\left(p,\vec{y}_{12}\right)^{\mu}
,
\vec{y}_{12}=\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}
, and
k_{ij}=k_{i}+k_{j}=|\vec{k}_{i}|+|\vec{k}_{j}|
. The loop integral needs to be regularized
Chowdhury et al. (2023)
, but we keep this aside for illustration
3
3

3

We discuss the regularized expression in Appendices
A
and
D
.
. Note that the integrand factorizes into a 4d Lorentz-invariant integrand dressed with an auxiliary integral with poles corresponding to external energies of the diagram. This simplicity arises from non-trivial cancellations after summing over two diagrams in the shadow formalism and does not occur for wavefunction coefficients (see appendix
E
for a discussion of wavefunction coefficients from this point of view). These simplifications can also be understood from non-trivial interference of the phases derived in
Sleight and Taronna (2021a)
;
Sleight and Taronna (2021b)
.

Let us now take a step back and think about how to directly arrive at (
9
) without having to sum over different diagrams.
We recall that energy is not conserved in de Sitter space since the metric has non-trivial time dependence. We may then naively guess that a physical observable can be obtained by taking a flat space Feynman diagram and dressing it with auxiliary propagators which encode the non-conservation of energy at each vertex:

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(10)

where
p
is a one-dimensional variable that encodes the energy of
K^{\mu}
as defined below (
9
), and we impose energy conservation at the point where the two auxiliary propagators meet. At this point, it is not obvious that such a prescription will lead to anything physically sensible, but looking at (
9
) we see that there is indeed a simple choice of auxiliary propagator which yields something observable, notably

\frac{k}{p^{2}+k^{2}},

(11)

and the observable in question is precisely the in-in correlator! The main point of this paper is to show that this is not an accident of a particular diagram or the conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
theory. Rather, we find that that in-in correlators for conformally coupled and massless scalar theories with more general polynomial interactions can be obtained by dressing flat space Feynman diagrams with simple auxiliary propagators, although the particular form of the propagators is theory-dependent. Note that the auxiliary propagators do not actually describe propagating degrees of freedom, but we nevertheless refer to them in this way because of their resemblance to one-dimensional Feynman propagators.

As another illustration of this point, let us next consider the tree-level 4-point in-in correlator in the conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory. Using the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism, this was found to be a sum of two terms
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
:

\mathcal{A}_{4}=2\mathcal{T}_{1}+\mathcal{T}_{2},

(12)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2y_{12}}\Big[\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{34}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{12}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\log\frac{k_{12}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\log\frac{k_{34}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Big],

(13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2y_{12}},

(14)

where
y_{12}=\left|\vec{y}_{12}\right|
For illustration let us focus on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and show that it can be obtained by dressing a flat space Feynman diagram with certain auxiliary propagators. We will see in the next section that
\mathcal{T}_{2}
arises by dressing of a flat space diagram as well. In particular,
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is given by the following dressed flat space Feynman diagram:

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{91.033pt}{30.47743pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 125.96 42.17)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{2}\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{34})^{2}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\,.\end{split}

(15)

From this we read off the the following form of the auxiliary propagators required for the conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory:

\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s+k_{\rm ext})^{2}}\,.

(16)

The integral over
s
leads to a log and since the diagram in (
15
) contains two such auxiliary propagators this evaluates to a dilog. This suggests that the transcendentality of the in-in correlator can be read off from the number of log-type auxiliary propagators. More precisely, it is given by the transcendentality of the flat space Feynman diagram plus the number of log-type auxiliary propagators. In the conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
theory, the auxiliary propagators do not have an
s
integral so one may expect that the in-in correlators have the same transcendentality as flat space Feynman diagrams and this is indeed the case for known examples
Chowdhury et al. (2023)
. Hence, the dressing rules not only provide the relation of in-in correlators to scattering amplitudes, but make their analytic structure manifest.

3
Dressing rules

In this section, we will spell out the dressing rules more systematically and illustrate how they are derived from the shadow formalism for a number of simple examples. A general derivation of the dressing rules can be found in appendix
B
.

We claim that in-in correlators can be obtained by summing over flat space Feynman diagrams dressed with certain one-dimensional auxiliary propagators attached to the interactions vertices. As we will see shortly, a dressed flat space diagram with
L
loops arises from summing over
2^{L}
diagrams in EAdS using the shadow formalism. Hence, at tree-level there are an equal number of dressed diagrams and shadow diagrams but at loop-level the dressed diagrams arise from very non-trivial cancellations. The precise form of the auxiliary propagators will be theory-dependent, but the following algorithm for computing in-in correlators from dressed flat space diagrams can be used in general:

1.

Write down a flat space Feynman diagram and relax energy conservation, i.e.
k_{\rm ext}+p_{\rm tot}=0
at the vertices, where
k_{\rm ext}
and
p_{\rm tot}
are defined in (
)
4
4

4

We will use the convention where each flat space propagator comes with a factor
\frac{1}{\pi}
.

2.

Attach an auxiliary propagator to each vertex and set the energy of each auxiliary propagator to be
p_{\rm tot}
of the vertex it is attached to.

3.

Attach the other end of all the auxiliary propagators to a single point, at which energy is conserved.

4.

In general there will be two types of auxiliary propagators (except in the conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
theory, where there is only one type). We will denote the two types using dashed and dotted lines, respectively. Sum over all ways of attaching the two types of auxiliary propagators subject to simple constraints that we specify in Table
3
.

5.

Integrate over all the unfixed energy variables.

In the final step, we can treat the integrals over the unfixed energy variables as contour integrals, summing over residues of the poles in the upper half of the complex plane. The remaining integrals will be over the boundary components of loop momenta. In what follows we will illustrate these rules for a variety of scalar field theories. We provide a summary of the dressing rules in Table
3
for the benefit of the reader.

3.1
Conformally coupled
\phi^{4}

For
V(\phi)=\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}
, the shadow action given in (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}L=-\left((\partial\phi_{+})^{2}-m^{2}{\phi_{+}}^{2}\right)+\left((\partial\phi_{-})^{2}-m^{2}{\phi_{-}}^{2}\right)-\frac{\lambda}{12}(\phi_{-}^{4}+\phi_{+}^{4})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\phi_{+}^{2}\phi_{-}^{2}.\end{split}

(17)

From this we can identify the Feynman rules for this theory. We find it convenient to absorb the symmetry factors due to the
m!
ways of contracting identical fields at a
\phi^{m}
vertex, leading to the Feynman vertex rules shown in Table
1
.

Vertex

\phi_{-}^{4}

\phi_{+}^{2}\phi_{-}^{2}

\phi_{+}^{4}

Factor

-2\lambda

2\lambda

-2\lambda

Table 1
:
The Feynman vertex rules for conformally coupled
\phi^{4}
.

(18)

where the dot refers to an interaction vertex to which four non-auxiliary propagators are attached,
p_{tot}
is the sum of the internal energies flowing into the vertex, and
k_{ext}
is the sum of external energies flowing into the vertex. See (
) for more details.

We have already seen how this rule emerges at 1-loop in section
2.2
, so let us now apply it to a more complicated example, notably the 2-loop necklace depicted below. In this case there are three vertices so we must attach three auxiliary propagators. Since the middle vertex is not attached to any external propagators, we have
k_{\rm ext}=0
so the auxiliary propagator attached to it reduces to
\pi\delta(p_{\rm tot})
, i.e. energy is conserved at this vertex just like in flat space. We subsequently obtain (modulo regularization)

\vec{k_{1}}
\vec{k_{2}}
\vec{k_{3}}
\vec{k_{4}}
\omega_{1}+p
\omega_{1}
\omega_{2}+p
\omega_{2}
p
p
0

\begin{split}=&-\frac{8}{\pi^{3}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\frac{k_{12}k_{34}}{(p^{2}+k_{12}^{2})(p^{2}+k_{34}^{2})}\int d^{4}L_{1}\,d^{4}L_{2}\frac{1}{L_{1}^{2}(L_{1}+P)^{2}L_{2}^{2}(L_{2}+P)^{2}}\,,\end{split}

(19)

where
P^{\mu}=(p,\vec{y}_{12})^{\mu}
and
L_{i}^{\mu}=(\omega_{i},\vec{q}_{i})^{\mu}
. The dashed propagator attaching to the middle vertex has zero energy since energy-momentum is conserved at that vertex. The modification of (
19
) for a de Sitter-invariant UV-regularization can be found in equation (
175
).

This result was derived using the shadow formalism in
Chowdhury et al. (2023)
, where the simple flat space integrand was obtained from nontrivial cancellations after summing over four different diagrams. This illustrates the power of the dressing rules.

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-\pi

(21)

of which there can be any number.

Even though the propagator in (
21
) has no energy dependence, we must still associate an energy to it that will enter after imposing energy conservation at each vertex following the algorithm in the beginning of this section. Note that the propagator in (
20
) gives rise to a logarithm after performing the
s
-integral. We may therefore expect that attaching such a propagator to a flat space Feynman diagram increases the transcendentality by one. We will see below and in later sections that this is indeed the case. For example, adding two such auxiliary propagators to a tree-level exchange diagram in flat space gives a dilogarithm. The auxiliary propagator in (
21
) also increases the transcendentality by one, but in a more trivial sense. When we discuss the transcendentality of correlators in what follows, we refer to their functional form rather than overall factors of
\pi
.

Let us illustrate how these dressing rules arise for a few simple examples starting from the shadow effective action.

Tree-level 3-point

For
V(\phi)=\frac{\lambda}{3!}\phi^{3}
, the shadow action (
5
) features no
\phi_{-}^{3}
vertex in
d=3
dimensions; therefore, this diagram does not naively exist. However, if one perturbs the boundary dimension to
d\to d+2\epsilon
and the scaling dimension
\Delta_{\pm}\to\Delta_{\pm}+\epsilon
5
5

5

We perturb the scaling dimension to ensure that
\Delta_{\pm}-\frac{d}{2}
is unchanged. This combination appears as the order of the Bessel functions in the propagators (see appendix
A
). For convenience, in a
\phi^{v}
potential we shift to
d=3+\frac{2}{v-2}\epsilon
such that the overall power of
z
has a coefficient
+1
on
\epsilon
. We henceforth leave this implicit.
then the Lagrangian in (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}L=-\left((\partial\phi_{+})^{2}-m^{2}{\phi_{+}}^{2}\right)&+\left((\partial\phi_{-})^{2}-m^{2}{\phi_{-}}^{2}\right)\\
&-\frac{\lambda\pi\epsilon}{2}\big(\frac{1}{3}\phi_{-}^{3}-\phi_{-}\phi_{+}^{2}\big)+\lambda\big(\frac{1}{3}\phi_{+}^{3}-\phi_{-}^{2}\phi_{+}\big).\end{split}

(22)

As in the
\phi^{4}
case, we obtain the Feynman rules after accounting for symmetry factors at the vertex, which are given in Table
2
.
The contact Witten diagram is displayed in Fig.
2(a)
and, using the propagators given in (
7
), evaluates to

\mathcal{A}_{3}=-\pi\epsilon\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,\frac{(z^{1+\epsilon})^{3}}{z^{4+2\epsilon}}e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3})z}=-\pi\lambda k^{-\epsilon}\epsilon\Gamma(\epsilon)\xrightarrow{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}-\pi\lambda\,,

(23)

which agrees with the result obtained in
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
. Note that despite the vertex being a suppressed one, we obtain a non-zero result because the
\epsilon
from the interaction vertex cancels the
\epsilon^{-1}
from the
z
-integral.

On the other hand, we may dress the flat space vertex applying (
21
) as in Figure

and can apply the dressing rules. We can only use the auxiliary propagator (
21
) since the one in (
20
) must occur an even number of times.
Hence the dressed three-point diagram is simply

\mathcal{A}_{3}=-\pi\lambda\,,

(24)

in agreement with (
23
).

Tree-level 4-point

Now let us consider the tree-level 4-point correlator. Focusing on the s-channel, there are two diagrams to consider in the shadow formalism, which are depicted in Figure 3.
In Figure
3(a)
, the internal propagator is
\phi^{+}
, and therefore we have two vertices of the type
(\phi^{-})^{2}\phi^{+}
. Table
2
tells us that the vertex factors are unsuppressed for
\epsilon\to 0
and are simply
-2\lambda
. Using the propagators given in (
7
) then gives

\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{4}=-\frac{(-2\lambda)^{2}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}\,dz_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\frac{1}{p^{2}+\vec{y}_{12}^{2}}\frac{e^{-k_{12}z_{1}}e^{-k_{34}z_{2}}}{z_{1}z_{2}}\sin(pz_{1})\sin(pz_{2})\,,

(25)

\vec{k_{1}}
\vec{k_{2}}

Figure 3
:
Shadow diagrams contributing to the four-point correlator at tree level in conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
. Here
\vec{y}_{12}=\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}.

where
\vec{y}_{ij}=\vec{k}_{i}+\vec{k}_{j}
and
k_{ij}=|\vec{k}_{i}|+|\vec{k}_{j}|
.

We shall now show that this expression arises from dressing a flat space Feynman diagram
by transforming the
z
-integral into an
s
-integral via the identities

z^{-n}e^{-kz}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,s^{n-1}e^{-(s+k)z}

(26)

and

\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,e^{-(s+k)z}\sin(pz)=\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s+k)^{2}}.

(27)

The identity (
26
) will be used repeatedly below. Applying each of these identities in turn (with
n=1
) gives

\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,\frac{e^{-kz}}{z}\sin(pz)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}dze^{-(k+s)z}\sin(pz)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\frac{p}{(s+k)^{2}+p^{2}}~,

(28)

and therefore (
25
) becomes

\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{4}=-\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{2}\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{34})^{2}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}.

(29)

As shown in appendix
C
, this representation allows us to express the integrals in a form which can be recognized as Hypergeometric functions.

We can now see that this is equivalent to the dressed diagram shown in Fig.
4(a)
with the choice of auxiliary propagator put forward in (
20
). Indeed, applying the dressing rules yields

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{4}=&\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\bigg(-2i\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\,\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\bigg)\bigg(-2i\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{2}\frac{p}{p^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{34})^{2}}\bigg)\\
&\times\Big(\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\Big),\end{split}

(30)

which is clearly the same as (
29
). In this instance, one may integrate to obtain

\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{4}=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{y_{12}}\Bigg[\text{Li}_{2}\bigg(\frac{k_{12}-y_{12}}{k_{12}+k_{34}}\bigg)+\text{Li}_{2}\bigg(\frac{k_{34}-y_{12}}{k_{12}+k_{34}}\bigg)+\ln\bigg|\frac{k_{12}+y_{12}}{k_{12}+k_{34}}\bigg|\ln\bigg|\frac{k_{34}+y_{12}}{k_{12}+k_{34}}\bigg|-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Bigg].

(31)

We now turn to the second exchange diagram contributing in the shadow formalism given in Fig.
3(b)
. This diagram requires careful handling of
\epsilon
factors. On the one hand, each vertex factor is linear in
\epsilon
. On the other, each
z
integral gives a
1/\epsilon
which exactly cancels the factors of
\epsilon
in the vertices. Applying the Feynman rules to the diagram in Figure
3(b)
yields

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{4}=\pi\epsilon^{2}\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}\,dz_{2}\frac{e^{-k_{12}z_{1}}e^{-k_{34}z_{2}}}{(z_{1}z_{2})^{1-\epsilon}}\cos(pz_{1})\cos(pz_{2})\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\,.

(32)

By performing the
z
-integrals we see that the leading contribution is just
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}
. This all we require due to the
\epsilon^{2}
prefactor.
The resulting integral in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit is therefore simply

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{4}=\lambda^{2}\pi\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\,\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}.

(33)

We recognise this to be a tree-level Feynman diagram integrated over the energy flowing through the propagator.

On the other hand, we have the dressed diagram with dotted auxiliary propagators, shown in Fig.
4(b)
. Applying the dressing rules here gives

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{4}=\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\,(-\pi)(-\pi)\Big(\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\Big)

(34)

which matches the result from the shadow computation, (
33
). This integral is simple and yields

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{4}=\lambda^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}}{y_{12}}.

(35)

The sum
\mathcal{A}_{4}=\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{4}+\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{4}
indeed matches the result found in
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
.

6
6

6

To avoid excessive notation, and since we shall not use them together,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is used to refer both to the one-loop diagram here and contact diagram above. It shall be clear from the context which diagram we mean.

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{3}&=\frac{4\epsilon\lambda^{3}}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{3}q\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\,dp_{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}\,dz_{2}\,dz_{3}\,(z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})^{\epsilon-1}e^{-k_{1}z_{1}}e^{-k_{2}z_{2}}e^{-k_{3}z_{3}}\\
&\times\Bigg(\sin(p_{3}z_{1})\sin(p_{3}z_{3})\cos(p_{1}z_{1})\cos(p_{1}z_{2})\cos(p_{2}z_{2})\cos(p_{2}z_{3})+(-1)^{2}\big(\sin\leftrightarrow\cos\big)\Bigg)\\
&\times\frac{1}{p_{3}^{2}+\vec{q}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{1}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{k_{1}})^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{2}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{y}_{12})^{2}},\end{split}\,,

(36)

where we have made explicit that the sign difference due to ghost-like propagators was required to cancel the sign difference due to different vertex factors. We can simplify the expression in the brackets by noticing the integrand is invariant under sign change of
p_{i}
and using standard trigonometric identities to obtain

\Bigg(\cdots\Bigg)=\sin\big((p_{3}-p_{2})z_{3}\big)\sin\big((p_{3}+p_{1})z_{1}\big)\cos\big((p_{1}+p_{2})z_{2}\big).

(37)

From this form it is easy to see that only the
z_{2}
integral in (
36
) is divergent. Evaluating this we have

\begin{split}\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{2}\,z_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}e^{-kz}\cos((pz_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\Gamma(\epsilon)\big((k-ip)^{\epsilon}+(k+ip)^{\epsilon}\big)\xrightarrow{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}1.\end{split}

(38)

Then, using (
26
) and

\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,e^{-sz}\sin(pz)=\frac{p}{p^{2}+s^{2}}\,,

(39)

the remaining integrals over
z_{1}
and
z_{3}
give

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{3}\\
&=\frac{4\lambda^{3}}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{3}q\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\,dp_{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{2}\frac{p_{3}+p_{1}}{(p_{3}+p_{1})^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{1})^{2}}\frac{p_{3}-p_{2}}{(p_{3}-p_{2})^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{3})^{2}}\\
&\times\frac{1}{p_{3}^{2}+\vec{q}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{1}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{k_{1}})^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{2}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{y}_{12})^{2}}.\end{split}

(40)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{3}\\

(41)

where

,

, and

. This matches (

) after making the following replacements in that equation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{1}&\rightarrow-p-\omega,\\
p_{2}&\rightarrow p+p^{\prime}+\omega,\\
p_{3}&\rightarrow\omega,\end{split}

(42)

such that
p_{3}+p_{1}\rightarrow-p
and
p_{3}-p_{2}\rightarrow-p-p^{\prime}
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{3}&=-\epsilon^{3}\lambda^{3}\int d^{3}q\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\,dp_{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}\,dz_{2}\,dz_{3}\,(z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})^{\epsilon-1}e^{-k_{1}z_{1}}e^{-k_{2}z_{2}}e^{-k_{3}z_{3}}\\

), we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{3}=-\lambda^{3}\int d^{3}q\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\,dp_{3}\,\frac{1}{p_{3}^{2}+\vec{q}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{1}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{k_{1}})^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{2}^{2}+(\vec{q}+\vec{y}_{12})^{2}}.\end{split}

and the dressing rule gives the following expression,

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{3}=&\lambda^{3}\int dp\,dp^{\prime}\int d^{3}q\,d\omega(-\pi)(-\pi)(-\pi)\\

3.3
Massless theories

Here we focus on correlators of massless fields with
\phi^{3}
and
\phi^{4}
interactions. These correlators exhibit IR divergences, which we will regulate using dimensional regularization, setting the boundary dimension to be
d=3+2\epsilon
. For a simplified example of applying IR-divergent dressing rules and performing all integrals, the interested reader should see the supplementary Mathematica file available in
[
1
]
. For a derivation of the auxiliary propagators presented here, see appendix
B
.

Apart from the
\epsilon
-dependence, the auxiliary propagators for these theories take a similar form to those of conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
except that they have nontrivial numerators. We therefore define the following numerators:

\mathcal{N}_{v}(s,\{k\},\{p\},\epsilon)=\frac{s^{-\epsilon}}{a_{M}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{i+j=v}\frac{a_{j}b_{i}s^{3-i-j}}{\Gamma(4-i-j-\epsilon)}\big((s+k_{\rm ext})\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}j)+p_{\rm tot}\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}j)\big)

(46)

and

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{v}(s,\{k\},\{p\},\epsilon)=\frac{s^{-\epsilon}}{a_{M}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{i+j=v}\frac{a_{j}b_{i}s^{3-i-j}}{\Gamma(4-i-j-\epsilon)}\big(p_{\rm tot}\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}j)-(s+k_{\rm ext})\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}j)\big),

(47)

where
v
is the valency of the interaction vertex,

a_{j}=\sum_{l_{1}<\ldots<l_{j}}p_{l_{1}}\ldots p_{l_{j}},\,\,\,\,b_{j}=\sum_{l_{1}<\ldots<l_{j}}k_{l_{1}}\ldots k_{l_{j}}\,,

(48)

and the
p
and
k
variables are energies of internal and external propagators attached to the vertex, respectively. If a vertex has
i
bulk-boundary propagators and
j
bulk-bulk propagators attached,
b_{i+m}=a_{j+m}=0
for
m>0
.
Moreover
M
is the number of internal propagators at the vertex and

a_{M}=\prod_{j=1}^{M}p_{j};\qquad a_{j}=0\,\text{ if }\,j>M.

(49)

Using the above definitions, the auxiliary propagators for a massless
\phi^{v}
theory are

•

dashed auxiliary propagator

\displaystyle\hbox to42.53pt{\vbox to23.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-49.8pt\lower-2.33395pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

dotted auxiliary propagator

7

As in the conformally coupled case, one of the auxiliary propagators has a factor of
i
which may seem unusual. The auxiliary propagators do not have a lagrangian description, hence the normalisation has no physical significance. Rather, it is used to ensure the correct relative factors between contributions to the same correlator, without having to introduce additional confusing rules.

(51)

of which there must be an even number.

Note that the coefficient of the auxiliary propagator in (
51
) vanishes as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, but this will be balanced by IR divergences coming from the
s
integral, giving a non-zero finite contribution. On the other hand, the auxiliary propagator in (
50
) will give rise to
1/\epsilon
terms, and therefore encodes IR divergences arising from the expansion of spacetime.

The dressing rules for all the theories we consider are summarised in table
3
. Analogous dressing rules for the wavefunction are discussed in Appendix
E
. As shown in the appendix, the dressing rules for the wave function contain denominators that explicitly violate Lorentz invariance and hence are not as simple as the dressing rules for in-in correlators
8

See
Lee (2023)
for a similar observation which pointed out that the poles of the in-in correlator overlap with the poles of the S-matrix.
. We will conclude this section by illustrating the massless dressing rules for a simple example and matching it with the shadow formalism.

For completeness, let us state the shadow action and subsequent Feynman rules for massless
\phi^{3}
and
\phi^{4}
theory. Similarly to (
22
), we now set
d=3+2\epsilon
which gives
\Delta_{-}=\epsilon
, and
\Delta_{+}=3+\epsilon
. Previously there were IR divergences only at intermediate steps but the final result was finite. Here we shall see that the IR divergences persist and are regulated by
\epsilon
in the final result. Using these values in (
5
) with
V(\phi)=\frac{\lambda}{v!}\phi^{v}
gives the

\phi^{3}
action,

\displaystyle\begin{split}L=(\partial\phi_{+})^{2}-(\partial\phi_{-})^{2}&+\lambda\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\big(\phi_{-}\phi_{+}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\phi_{-}^{3}\big)\\
&+\lambda\sin\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\big(\frac{1}{3}\phi_{+}^{3}-\phi_{-}\phi_{+}^{2}\big).\end{split}

(52)

with Feynman rules shown in Table
4
. Similarly, the action for the massless
\phi^{4}
theory takes a similar form,

\displaystyle\begin{split}L=(\partial\phi_{+})^{2}-(\partial\phi_{-})^{2}&-\frac{\lambda\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)}{2}\big(\frac{1}{6}\phi_{-}^{4}-\phi_{-}^{2}\phi_{+}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\phi_{+}^{4}\big)\\
&+\frac{\lambda\sin\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)}{3}\big(\phi_{-}^{3}\phi_{+}-\phi_{-}\phi_{+}^{3}\big).\end{split}

(53)

for which the Feynman rules are shown in Table
5
. Note that we do not take
\epsilon\to 0
at this step. Due to the IR divergences in this theory, subleading terms in
\cos(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2})
and
\sin(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2})
can lead to finite contributions when one eventually expands in small
\epsilon
.

Comparing the conformally coupled actions in (
17
) and (
22
) to the massless actions in (
52
) and (
53
), we see that the
\phi_{-}
and
\phi_{+}
fields have swapped roles. For example, the
\phi_{-}^{3}
vertex is suppressed in the conformally coupled theory but unsuppressed in the massless theory. Furthermore, the signs of the kinetic terms have swapped between the two cases. This is why the rules governing the number of dashed versus dotted auxiliary propagators given in Table
3
are opposite for massless and conformally coupled theories.

Tree-level 3-point

Let us consider the tree-level 3-point correlator in massless
\phi^{3}
theory. From the Feynman rules in Table
4
we see that there is a single shadow diagram, which is shown in Figure
9
.

:
The three-point contact diagram in massless

from the shadow formalism and the dressing rule.

Using (
7
) and the Feynman rule for the
\left(\phi^{-}\right){}^{3}
vertex in Table
4
, we find

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{3}&=-2\lambda\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\int dz\,z^{-4+\epsilon}(1+k_{1}z)(1+k_{2}z)(1+k_{3}z)e^{-k_{t}z}\\
&=-2\lambda\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\int dz\,\sum_{i=0}^{3}b_{i}z^{-4+i+\epsilon}e^{-k_{123}z}.\end{split}

(54)

Using (
26
) then gives

\mathcal{A}_{3}=-2\lambda\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{1}{s+k_{123}}\frac{b_{i}s^{3-i-\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-i-\epsilon)}.

(55)

On the other hand, if we consider the dressed diagram in Figure
9
with the auxiliary propagator given by (
46
) and (
50
) with
p_{j}=0
we obtain

\mathcal{A}_{3}=\lambda\Big(-2\cos\left(\frac{\pi\epsilon}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{1}{(s+k_{123})^{2}}\frac{b_{i}s^{3-i-\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-i-\epsilon)}(s+k_{123})\Big)~,

(56)

which agrees with the result from shadow (
55
). We will explain how to evaluate this integral and integrals for other massless tree-level diagrams in section
5
. A general derivation of the dressing rules for massless correlators is given in Appendix
B
.

4
Conformally coupled 5-point

To illustrate the power of the dressing rules, in this section we will compute for the first time the tree-level 5-point in-in correlator in conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory. The Feynman diagrams contributing to the corresponding 5-point amplitude have three interactions vertices, and therefore according to the dressing rules we expect to have four dressings for each flat space Feynman diagram. In particular, since dashed auxiliary propagators must appear in pairs, we can dress a flat space Feynman diagram with three dotted auxiliary propagators, or we can dress it in three different ways with two dashed auxiliary propagators and one dotted auxiliary propagator.

The first dressed diagram we consider is

&=\frac{\lambda^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\,ds_{3}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{p_{2}^{2}+(s_{3}+k_{34})^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{1}^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{2}^{2}+y_{34}^{2}}=0\end{split}

(57)

where
s_{1}
is the variable associated to the left auxiliary propagator, and
\vec{y}_{ij}=\vec{k}_{i}+\vec{k}_{j}
. This integral is zero because each
p
integrand is an odd function. The second dressed diagram we consider is

&=\frac{\lambda^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{2}\,ds_{3}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\frac{p_{1}-p_{2}}{(s_{2}+k_{5})^{2}+(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{(s_{3}+k_{34})^{2}+p_{2}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{1}^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}\frac{1}{p_{2}^{2}+y_{34}^{2}}\end{split}

(58)

which can be straightforwardly integrated as there are no IR divergences and the form of the integral is similar to 4-pt function (see (
29
)).

The third diagram is related to the previous one by relabelling:

\lxSVG@closescope }\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 103.78 0 L 103.78 69.19}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\mathcal{A}_{5}^{(2)}\Bigg|_{(12)\leftrightarrow(34)}.\end{split}

(59)

Hence we do not need to evaluate another integral. The sum of these two diagrams is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{5}^{(2)}+\mathcal{A}_{5}^{(3)}=\frac{\lambda^{3}\pi}{2y_{12}y_{34}}\Big(\log\frac{1-v_{-}}{u_{+}}\log(1-v_{-}-u_{+})+\log\frac{1-u_{-}}{v_{+}}\log(1-u_{-}-v_{+})\\
&\qquad\qquad+\log(1-u_{-}-v_{-})\log\frac{u_{+}v_{+}}{(1-u_{-})(1-v_{-})}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{u_{-}}{1-v_{-}}+\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{v_{-}}{1-u_{-}}-\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{v_{+}}{1-u_{-}}-\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{u_{+}}{1-v_{-}}\Big)\end{split}

(60)

where
u_{\pm}
and
v_{\pm}
are analogues of the cross ratios at five-point, defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}2k_{12}&=k_{12345}(u_{+}+u_{-}),\qquad 2k_{34}=k_{12345}(v_{+}+v_{-}),\\
2y_{12}&=k_{12345}(u_{+}-u_{-}),\qquad 2y_{34}=k_{12345}(v_{+}-v_{-})~.\end{split}

(61)

The final diagram we consider has only trivial auxiliary propagators and is easily evaluated:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{5}^{(4)}\equiv\hbox to154.23pt{\vbox to94.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1142pt\lower-44.01045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.8pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.8pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 103.78 0 L 103.78 69.19}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-\lambda^{3}\pi\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp_{1}\,dp_{2}\frac{1}{(p_{1}^{2}+y_{12}^{2})(p_{2}^{2}+y_{34}^{2})}\\
&=-\frac{\lambda^{3}\pi^{3}}{y_{12}y_{34}}.\end{split}

(62)

We see that although there were four diagrams, we only actually had to evaluate two integrals, one of which was trivial. The total correlator is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{5}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{5}^{(i)}+\text{perms}\\
=&\frac{\pi}{2y_{12}y_{34}}\Big(\log\frac{1-v_{-}}{u_{+}}\log(1-v_{-}-u_{+})+\log\frac{1-u_{-}}{v_{+}}\log(1-u_{-}-v_{+})\\
&\qquad\qquad+\log(1-u_{-}-v_{-})\log\frac{u_{+}v_{+}}{(1-u_{-})(1-v_{-})}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{u_{-}}{1-v_{-}}+\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{v_{-}}{1-u_{-}}-\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{v_{+}}{1-u_{-}}-\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{u_{+}}{1-v_{-}}-2\pi^{2}\Big)+\text{perms}\end{split}

(63)

where
+
perms means permutations over the external legs. We shall see section
6
that obtaining this result via the wavefunction is highly non-trivial, with large and complicated expressions vanishing in the analytic continuation. In particular, we shall find that the in-in correlator has lower transcendentality than the wavefunction, even at tree-level, and can be computed in a much simpler way by the dressing rules. Note that one could have guessed the appearance of the dilogarithm before evaluating the integral by the fact at most two non-trivial auxiliary propagators appear in any diagram.

5
Comparison to Schwinger-Keldysh formalism

In this section we will compare the correlators obtained via dressing rules to the correlators computed using Schwinger-Keldysh formalism, finding complete agreement, irrespective of the presence of IR-divergences.

5.1
Propagators

The two kinds of fields in Schwinger-Keldysh will be referred to as the time-ordered (T) and anti-time-ordered (A) fields. There are four kinds of bulk-bulk propagators which are often referred to as the Wightman functions. These are represented as
\Lambda_{TT}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})
,
\Lambda^{(\Delta)}_{AA}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})
,
\Lambda^{(\Delta)}_{AT}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})
and
\Lambda_{TA}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})
with
\Delta
denoting the conformal dimension of the exchanged fields. These arise via the Wick contractions between the time-ordered and anti-time-ordered fields. Their explicit expressions are given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{TT}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{d/2}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta)H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta^{\prime})+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta^{\prime})H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta)\Big],\\
\Lambda_{AA}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{d/2}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta^{\prime})H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta)+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta)H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta^{\prime})\Big],\\
\Lambda_{TA}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{d/2}\Big[H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta^{\prime})H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta)\Big],\\
\Lambda_{AT}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{d/2}\Big[H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta)H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta^{\prime})\Big]~,\end{split}

(64)

where
H
is a Hankel function,
\vec{k}
is the boundary momentum flowing through the propagators, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta^{2}=\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\Delta(\Delta-d)~.\end{split}

(65)

These will be denoted via the following diagrams

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{TT}^{(\Delta)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=\hbox to69.94pt{\vbox to36.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.26842pt\lower-18.13058pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

The bulk-boundary propagators are obtained by taking a limit of the bulk-bulk propagators. For example,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda^{(\Delta)}_{T}(\eta;\vec{k})&=\lim_{\eta^{\prime}\to 0}\eta^{\prime\beta-d/2}\Lambda^{(\beta)}_{TT}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})\propto\eta^{d/2}H_{\beta}^{(1)}(k\eta),\\
\Lambda^{(\Delta)}_{A}(\eta;\vec{k})&=\lim_{\eta^{\prime}\to 0}\eta^{\prime\beta-d/2}\Lambda^{(\beta)}_{AA}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})\propto\eta^{d/2}H_{\beta}^{(2)}(k\eta).\end{split}

(66)

We consider the three- and four-point functions for theories consisting of massless fields. For these fields with
d=3
and
\beta=\frac{3}{2}
, and the bulk-boundary propagators take the following form:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{T}^{(2)}(\eta;\vec{k})&=\eta e^{ik\eta},\qquad\Lambda_{A}^{(2)}(\eta;\vec{k})=\eta e^{-ik\eta},\\
\Lambda_{T}^{(3)}(\eta;\vec{k})&=(1-i\eta k)e^{i\eta k},\qquad\Lambda_{A}^{(3)}(\eta;\vec{k})=(1+i\eta k)e^{-i\eta k}~.\end{split}

(67)

Note that we are dropping an overall normalisation
k^{d-2\Delta_{-}}
in order to match the convention used in the previous section. The bulk-bulk propagators are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{TT}^{(3)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})(1+ik\eta)(1-ik\eta^{\prime})e^{-ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)(1-ik\eta)(1+ik\eta^{\prime})e^{ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}\Big]~,\\
\Lambda_{AA}^{(3)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})(1+ik\eta^{\prime})(1-ik\eta)e^{ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)(1+ik\eta)(1-ik\eta^{\prime})e^{-ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}\Big]~,\\
\Lambda_{TA}^{(3)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(1+ik\eta^{\prime})(1-ik\eta)e^{ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}~,\\
\Lambda_{AT}^{(3)}(\eta,\eta^{\prime};\vec{k})&=(1-ik\eta^{\prime})(1+ik\eta)e^{-ik(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}~.\end{split}

(68)

The deformation of
\Delta_{\pm}
and
d
enters the propagators only via powers of
\eta
, in analogy with (

). Furthermore, the arguments of the logarithms can be made dimensionless by accounting for the scale introduced by the regulator, which we suppress.
scheme as previous sections and, as will be shown below, the answer matches with the result obtained via dressing rules.

Using the dressing rules given in section
3
, there is only one diagram to consider,

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-2\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{1}{s+k_{123}}\frac{b_{i}s^{3-i-\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-i-\epsilon)}

(71)

where the
b_{i}
’s are defined in (
48
). For this case we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{0}=1,\quad b_{1}=k_{123},\quad b_{2}=k_{1}k_{2}+k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3},\quad b_{3}=k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}~.\end{split}

(72)

We may evaluate the
s
-integral directly to obtain

\mbox{\eqref{eq:3ptContactIRStart}}=2\lambda\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{b_{i}k_{123}^{3-i-\epsilon}\pi\;\text{cosec}(\pi(i+\epsilon))}{\Gamma(4-i-\epsilon)}.

(73)

One may then easily expand in small
\epsilon
and perform the sum to find that it agrees with (
69
) up to an unimportant normalisation factor.
The divergent term in (
73
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=0}^{3}(-1)^{i}\frac{b_{i}b_{1}^{3-i}}{(3-i)!\epsilon}=-\frac{b_{1}^{3}-3b_{1}b_{2}+3b_{3}}{3\epsilon}.\end{split}

(74)

Substituting in the values of
b_{i}
in terms of external energies reduces the numerator to
k_{123c}
.
We note that the divergent piece indeed agrees with that obtained using cutoff regularisation, for example using eqs. (3.8) and (2.22)of
Céspedes et al. (2024)
, if one associates
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
with a logarithm of the cutoff.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-2\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\sum_{i=0}^{4}\frac{b_{i}s^{3-i-\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-i-\epsilon)(s+k_{1234})}.\end{split}

(76)

By performing the integral manner similar to (
71
) we find that this agrees with (
75
). Furthermore, the leading divergence again agrees with the cutoff result; see eqs. (3.7) and (2.22) of
Céspedes et al. (2024)
.

5.4
Massless
\phi^{3}
4-point

68
). The four diagrams contributing to the correlator are

(77)

Using the techniques described in Appendix
C
, we can evaluate the integrals appearing in these diagrams to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{4}=-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\frac{k_{1234c}y_{12}^{3}+2k_{12c}k_{34c}}{y_{12}^{3}}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\epsilon y_{12}^{3}}\Bigg[-\left(y_{12}^{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)k_{34}k_{12c}-\left(y_{12}^{2}+k_{1}k_{2}\right)k_{12}k_{34c}-\frac{8}{3}k_{34c}k_{12c}-\frac{y_{12}Ek_{12c}k_{34c}}{k_{12}k_{34}}\\
&\qquad-\frac{7}{6}y_{12}^{3}\left(k_{12c}+k_{34c}\right)+\left(k_{12c}+y_{12}^{3}\right)k_{34c}\log(k_{12}+y_{12})+\left(k_{34c}+y_{12}^{3}\right)k_{12c}\log(k_{34}+y_{12})\\
&\qquad+\gamma_{E}\big\{y_{12}^{3}\left(k_{12c}+k_{34c}\right)+2k_{12c}k_{34c}\big\}\Bigg]+O(\epsilon^{0}),\end{split}

(78)

where we have only quoted the divergent parts. The finite part can be found in Appendix

C.2
.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
&=\frac{4\lambda^{2}\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}\epsilon)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\,ds_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\frac{\mathcal{N}_{3}(s_{1};k_{1},k_{2};p)}{p^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{3}(s_{2};k_{3},k_{4};p)}{p^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{34})^{2}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+\vec{y}_{12}^{2}},\end{split}

(79)

which will give the bulk of the structure, including all of the divergent pieces, logarithms, and dilogarithms in the finite piece.
\mathcal{N}_{3}
in the above is defined in (
46
). The
p
integral can be evaluated by summing over the residues of poles in the upper half plane (note that the pole at
p=0
is not included in the contour. See Appendix
A
for more details). We are then left with integrals over
s
parameters. See appendix
C
and the Mathematica code in
[
1
]
for a discussion of how to evaluate integrals of this form. Note that the finite piece has a transcendentality two, as expected since the dressed diagram has two dashed auxiliary propagators.

As in the conformally coupled case, the diagram with dotted propagators provides a finite contribution to the correlator. The diagram and its integral expression are:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.8pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.8pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 138.37 0 L 103.78 69.19}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
&=\frac{\lambda^{2}\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}\epsilon)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds_{1}\,ds_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{3}(s_{1};k_{1},k_{2};p)}{p^{2}+(s_{1}+k_{12})^{2}}\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{3}(s_{2};k_{3},k_{4};p)}{p^{2}+(s_{2}+k_{34})^{2}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y_{12}^{2}}.\end{split}

(80)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{3}
is defined in (
47
). Using the method of Appendix
C
, we find that this diagram is given by

\mbox{\eqref{eq:masslessdottedexchange}}=2\pi^{2}(2y_{12}^{3}+k_{1234c})+O(\epsilon),

(81)

which is finite. In contrast to the finite part of (
79
), this diagram does not contribute any terms of transcendental weight two. This further reinforces the observation that one can read off the transcendentality of tree-level correlators by counting the number of dashed auxiliary propagators, even in the massless case.

The sum of these diagrams matches the result obtained from the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism in (
77
). Hence, there is complete agreement between regularized correlators evaluated via dressing rules and the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism.

6
Comparison to wavefunction formalism

We review the computation of cosmological correlators from cosmological wavefunctions
Hartle and Hawking (1983)
. As reviewed in (
2
), this is defined by the following path integral:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{n}=\frac{\int D\phi|\Psi[\phi]|^{2}\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\phi(\vec{k}_{n})}{\int D\phi|\Psi[\phi]|^{2}},\end{split}

(82)

where
\phi(\vec{k})
denotes field insertions at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in dS. The coefficients of the wavefunction
\Psi[\phi]
in (
3
) can be computed by analytically continuing Witten diagrams in AdS
Maldacena (2003)
;
Maldacena and Pimentel (2011)
;
McFadden and Skenderis (2010)
.
Plugging (
3
) into (
82
), the correlator is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{2}&=\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k})},\\
\mathcal{A}_{3}&=\frac{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{3})}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{1})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{2})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{3})},\\
\mathcal{A}_{4}&=\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{4})}\Bigg\{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{4}(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{4})\\
&\quad+\frac{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(\vec{k}_{3},\vec{k}_{4},-\vec{k}_{1}-\vec{k}_{2})}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2})}+(1\leftrightarrow 4)+(1\leftrightarrow 3)\Bigg\},\end{split}

(83)

with similar expressions at higher points. In this section we match various correlators obtained via the wavefunction formalism and the dressing rules. We also demonstrate the simplicity of the cosmological correlator compared to the wavefunction coefficient at five points in the conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory and explain how this simplicity extends to any odd point function at tree level (see section
6.2
).

6.1
Conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
4-point

First we review the 4-point in-in correlator in conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory obtained from the wavefunction
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
and show how it relates to the result obtained from dressing rules.
The wavefunction can be computed from the following propagators in AdS:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(z,k)&=z^{3/2}K_{1/2}(kz),\\
G_{\frac{1}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=\frac{(z_{1}z_{2})^{3/2}}{2k}\Big[\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})K_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{1})I_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{2})+\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})I_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{2})K_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{1})\Big]~.\end{split}

(84)

We can then obtain the wavefunction in dS by performing the following analytical continuation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}l_{AdS}\to il_{dS},\qquad k\to-ik~.\end{split}

(85)

Since we have set
l_{AdS}=1
, the first analytical continuation can be absorbed by simply analytically continuing the coupling constant,
\lambda\to i\lambda
. Having performed the integrals and the analytical continuation we obtain the following expressions for
\Psi_{3}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{3})
and the
s
-channel contribution to
\Psi_{4}(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{4})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{4}^{(s)}&=\frac{1}{2y_{12}}\Big[\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{34}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{12}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\log\frac{k_{12}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\log\frac{k_{34}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Big],\\
\Psi_{3}&=-i\Big[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\log(ik_{123})\Big],\end{split}

(86)

where
\epsilon
is the regularization parameter. The expressions for other channels can be obtained by exchanging the momenta. To compute the cosmological correlator we require the real part of the wavefunction coefficients

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{Re}\Psi_{4}^{(s)}&=\frac{1}{2y_{12}}\Big[\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{34}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{12}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\log\frac{k_{12}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\log\frac{k_{34}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Big],\\
\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}&=\frac{\pi}{2}~.\end{split}

(87)

Note that
\Psi_{3}
is divergent but upon computing the real part, the divergence cancels out and the only contribution we receive is from the discontinuity in the logarithm, as given in (
87
). We will see a higher-point analogue of this mechanism in section
6.2
.

The real part of the 2-point wavefunction coefficient is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{k})=k.\end{split}

(88)

Using the above formulae and (
83
) we obtain the familiar expression for the 4-point cosmological correlator

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{4}&=\frac{1}{2y_{12}}\Big[\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{34}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\mbox{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{k_{12}-y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\right)+\log\frac{k_{12}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}\log\frac{k_{34}+y_{12}}{k_{1234}}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\Big].\end{split}

(89)

By comparing with the computation via dressing rules in section
3.2
we see that
\mbox{Re}\Psi_{4}^{(s)}
matches the diagram with two dashed auxiliary propagators whereas
\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}^{(s)}\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}^{(s)}
matches the diagram with two dotted auxiliary propagators.

6.2
Conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
5-point

The five point correlator in
\phi^{3}
theory can be expressed in terms of the wavefunction coefficients by using the path integral representation given in (
82
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{5}=\mbox{Re}\Psi_{5}(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{5})\\
&+\sum_{\{\pi\}}\frac{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{4}(\vec{k}_{\pi_{1}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{2}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{3}},\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(-\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{4}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{5}})}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}})}\\
&+\sum_{\{\pi\}}\frac{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(\vec{k}_{\pi_{1}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{2}},\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(-\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}},\vec{y}_{\pi_{4}\pi_{5}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{3}})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{3}(-\vec{y}_{\pi_{4},\pi_{5}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{4}},\vec{k}_{\pi_{5}})}{\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}})\mbox{Re}\Psi_{2}(\vec{y}_{\pi_{4}\pi_{5}})}~,\end{split}

(90)

where
\vec{y}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}}=\vec{k}_{\pi_{1}}+\vec{k}_{\pi_{2}}
and the sum is over various permutation of momenta denoting different channels.

The wavefunction coefficients appearing in the final answer have been previously computed
Arkani-Hamed and Maldacena (2015)
;
Hillman and Pajer (2022)
and can be expressed in terms of polylogs with maximal weight 3. In particular,
\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{4}
are computed in section

and
\Psi_{5}
is given in equation 5.8 of
Hillman and Pajer (2022)
and is computed using symbols
Goncharov et al. (2010)

with perms denoting the sum over other channels obtained by exchanging the momenta. The final result of this diagram involves polylogs of weight 3, namely, Li
3
, Li
2
Li
1
and Li
{}_{1}^{3}
along with polylogs of lower weight. In particular, it can be checked that the part of the answer that contains Li
3
simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{5}=-i\lambda^{3}\sum_{a,b=\pm}(-1)^{ab}\Big({\rm Li}_{3}(1-\frac{1-v_{a}}{u_{b}})+{\rm Li}_{3}(1-\frac{1-u_{b}}{v_{a}})+{\rm Li}_{3}(1-\frac{(1-v_{a})(1-u_{b})}{u_{b}v_{a}})\Big)+\cdots~,\end{split}

(92)

where the factor of
i
appears via the analytical continuation described in (
85
),
v_{\pm},u_{\pm}
are defined in (
61
), and
\cdots
denote the other terms in the answer, which contain polylogs of lower weight (see equation 5.8 of
Hillman (2019)
for the complete expression). The coefficients of the other weight 3 polylogs (i.e., Li
2
Li
1
and Li
{}_{1}^{3}
) also contain an overall factor of
i\lambda^{3}
.

Since the wavefunction contains functions with transcendentality weight three, one would naively expect the cosmological correlator to also have such functions. However, the correlator obtained in section
4
was found to contain functions of maximum weight two. This can be understood as follows.
Note that for physical momenta we have
\frac{1-v_{a}}{u_{b}},\frac{1-u_{b}}{v_{a}}>0
, which ensures that the arguments of Li
3
in (
92
) are
<1
and hence evaluate to real values. A similar statement is also true for the other polylogs of weight 3, i.e.,
{\rm Li}_{2}{\rm Li}_{1}
and
{\rm Li}_{1}^{3}
. Since each of these polylogs have an imaginary coefficient,
i\lambda^{3}
, upon computing
\mbox{Re}\Psi_{5}
, polylogs of weight 3 drop out! A similar simplification was also found for the 3-point function in equation (
87
) where the contribution to the in-in correlator appeared from the discontinuity of
\log
in equation (
86
). The relative simplicity of the three and five-point tree-level in-in correlators compared to wavefunction coefficients can be easily understood from the dressing rules given in Section
4
. Similar simplicity extends to all odd-point functions at tree-level.

6.3
Massless 2-point

Next we consider massless two-point functions and use the dressing rules to reproduce previous results obtained using more conventional methods. In doing so, we will see how free correlators arise from the dressing rules. We will also consider a 1-loop example, and show that the dressing rules reproduce all of the expected IR divergences.

Free 2-point function

The free 2-point wavefunction for massless scalar fields was computed in
Maldacena (2003)
by inserting two bulk-to-boundary propagators into the action and putting a cut-off in conformal time to regulate an IR divergence. While the divergence can be removed by adding a local counterterm in the CFT, it is imaginary and cancels out when computing the 2-point in-in correlator, which is obtained by taking the real part of the wavefunction and inverting it. The resulting expression is proportional to
k^{-3}
, where
k
is the energy of the external legs.

This result can be trivially obtained from the dressing rules in the following way. We first start with a trivial Feynman diagram in flat space which has no interaction vertices and a single external leg, which gives a factor of
1
. The lack of vertices means there are no auxiliary propagators to attach. We then recall from section
2.1
that we must restore a factor of
\eta_{0}^{\Delta_{-}}k^{2\Delta_{-}-d}
for each external leg in order to obtain the in-in correlator. In the present case,
\Delta_{-}=0
which yields
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\rm free}=k^{-3}
, as expected. Using similar reasoning, we find that the free 2-point correlator in the conformally coupled case is proportional to
1/k
, in agreement with standard results.

To understand why we consider a Feynman diagram with only a single leg, note that we can compute the free 2-point correlator by starting with a single bulk-boundary propagator and dragging the other end to the boundary, as depicted below.

Figure 10
:
Free 2-point function

Massless
\phi^{4}
tadpole

Let us now consider the 1-loop 2-point correlator in massless
\phi^{4}
theory, which can be derived from the dressed flat space diagram
in Fig.
11
. In equation (3.15) of
Céspedes et al. (2024)
, the leading IR divergence of this diagram using a cutoff was found to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{2}\sim\frac{\lambda}{k^{3}}\log(kL_{IR})\log(-2k\eta_{0})+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(93)

where
L_{IR}
is the loop momentum cutoff and
\eta_{0}
encodes the end of inflation. The dressing rules explicitly use dimensional regularisation, so to compare to this result we make the association
\log(\#\eta_{0})\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon}
.

The integral to be performed from the dressing rules in section
3.3
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lambda\int\!\!dp\int\limits_{L_{IR}}\!\!\frac{d^{3}y}{\pi p^{2}(p^{2}+\vec{y}^{2})}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dss^{-\epsilon}}{s+2k}\\
&\quad\times\left(\frac{k^{2}p^{2}}{s\Gamma(-\epsilon)}+\frac{k^{2}s}{\Gamma(2-\epsilon)}+\frac{2kp^{2}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}+\frac{2ks^{2}}{\Gamma(3-\epsilon)}+\frac{p^{2}s}{\Gamma(2-\epsilon)}+\frac{s^{3}}{\Gamma(4-\epsilon)}\right).\end{split}

(94)

Note that this is both UV and IR divergent. The UV divergence can be regulated using a UV cutoff or analytic regularisation (see Appendix
A.2
for more details on the latter), but we will neglect this divergence for simplicity. Performing the
p
integral via residues and regulating the
\vec{y}
integral with a cutoff then gives the leading divergence

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sim\lambda\frac{k^{3}\log(kL_{IR})}{\epsilon}\,,\end{split}

(95)

which matches (
93
) after restoring a factor of
k^{2\Delta_{-}-d}
for each external leg.

\vec{k}
\vec{k}
p_{tot}=0

:
Dressed tadpole diagram.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\frac{3}{2}}(z,k)&=z^{3/2}K_{3/2}(kz),\\
G_{\frac{3}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=\frac{1}{2k}(z_{1}z_{2})^{3/2}\Big[\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})K_{\frac{3}{2}}(kz_{1})I_{\frac{3}{2}}(kz_{2})+\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})I_{\frac{3}{2}}(kz_{2})K_{\frac{3}{2}}(kz_{1})\Big]~.\end{split}
